The final configuration to check is when
p=(i,j)
and
q=(i,k)
with
k=j+1
. Consider the diagrams
W^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}\setminus p)\cup r=(j,j+2),[n])
and
W^{\prime\prime}=(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathcal{P}\setminus p)\cup s=(k-2,k),[n])
. Since the edge
r\not\in\mathcal{P}
(it would cross
q
if it were), and
s\not\in\mathcal{P}
(it would cross
p
if it were), we see that
W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime\prime}
satisfy both the non-crossing and density conditions, and thus are admissible. Furthermore,
W
,
W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime\prime}
are in the configurations laid out in display (
8
) and the diagrams
W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime\prime}
are in the configuration laid out in Case 2a.

We see from Lemma
A.4
that pole defined by the limit of sending
(x_{p,i}x_{q,i+1}-x_{p,i+1}x_{q,i})
to
0
cancels with degree one poles in the diagrams in
W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime\prime}
under the correct parameterization:

\displaystyle\lim_{(x_{p,i}x_{q,i+1}-x_{p,i+1}x_{q,i})\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))+\lim_{x_{r,k-2}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}))+\lim_{x_{s,j+3}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}))=0\;.

The limits of
I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}))
and
I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime}))
are both codimension one. By Corollary
A.10
, we see that the limit of
I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
is as well.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Since this cancellation was demonstrated at point by point level, it holds not only on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R},\geq 0}(k,n)
, but also on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k,n)
.
However, we remark that this cancellation is exact only in the space parameterized by matrices of the form
M(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
, as a subspace of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k,n)
and not in the space parameterized by matrices of the form
M(\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}))
, as a subspace of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k,n+1)
. In
[
7
,
19
]
, the authors explicitly show that cancellations of this form do not hold in the larger space because of orientation issues.

Appendix A
Pole Cancellation calculations

In this section, we present some useful calculations for understanding of the cancellation of spurious poles. Many of the results here can be found in
[
20
,
18
,
7
]
. However, they are presented here for completeness.

Recall from Definition
3.7
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))(\mathcal{Z}_{*})=\int_{(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{4})^{k}}\frac{\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\prod_{v\in V_{p}}dx_{p,v}}{R(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))}\delta^{4k|4k}(M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P})}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{*})

where, for
X
a
k\times k+4
matrix,

\displaystyle\delta^{4k|4k}(X)=\prod_{b=1}^{k}(X_{b,4+b})^{4}\delta^{4}((X_{b,1},X_{b,2},X_{b,3},X_{b,4}))\;.

Write
\mathcal{Z}_{*}^{i}
to indicate the
i^{th}
column of
\mathcal{Z}_{*}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{*}^{\mu}
to indicate the matrix formed by taking the first 4 columns of
\mathcal{Z}_{*}
. Then evaluating the integral
I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
corresponds to localizing the expression

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{b=1}^{k}(Y_{b}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{*}^{b})^{4}}{R(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))}

at the solution to
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P})}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{*}^{\mu}=0
. Writing the propagator
p=(i,j)
with
i<j
, Cramer’s rule implies that this localization evaluates to

\displaystyle x_{p,0}

\displaystyle=\det(Z_{i}^{\mu},Z_{i+1}^{\mu},Z_{j}^{\mu},Z_{j+1}^{\mu})

(5)

\displaystyle x_{p,i}=\det(Z_{0}^{\mu},Z_{i+1}^{\mu},Z_{j}^{\mu},Z_{j+1}^{\mu})\;

\displaystyle;\;x_{p,i+1}=\det(Z_{i}^{\mu},Z_{0}^{\mu},Z_{j}^{\mu},Z_{j+1}^{\mu})\;\text{ etc.}

(6)

That is, the entry
x_{p,m}
evaluates to the minor of
\mathcal{Z}_{*}^{\mu}
indicated by the rows in
V_{p}
, with the
m^{th}
row replaced by
Z_{0}^{\mu}
.

Lemma A.1
.

For two propagators
p=(i,j)
and
q=(i,j+1)
, after localization
x_{p,j}=-x_{q,j+2}
.

Proof.

By the above arguments, note that
x_{p,j}=\det(Z_{i}^{\mu},Z_{i+1}^{\mu},Z_{0}^{\mu},Z_{j+1}^{\mu})
while
x_{q,j+2}=\det(Z_{i}^{\mu},Z_{i+1}^{\mu},Z_{j+1}^{\mu},Z_{0}^{\mu})
. Thus these two values are negatives of each other.
∎

Sometimes, as in Theorem
3.13
and Theorem
4.5
, it is necessary to perform changes of variables in order to understand the relevant loci. In particular, we need the following simplifying change of variables:

Lemma A.2
.

Consider two propagators
p=(i,j)
and
q=(i,k)
that are adjacent on the
i^{th}
edge of a Wilson loop diagram
(\mathcal{P},[n])
, with
p
appearing closer to the vertex
i
and
q
closer to the vertex
i+1
. There is a reparameterization of the matrix
M_{\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P})}
under which one can replace the factor
x_{p,i}(x_{p,i}x_{q,i+1}-x_{p,i+1}x_{q,i})x_{q,i+1}
in
R(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
with the product of 4 terms:
xyzw
.

Proof.

We restrict our attention to the relevant two by two minor of
M_{\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P})}
,
\begin{bmatrix}x_{p,i}&x_{p,i+1}\\
x_{q,i}&x_{q,i+1}\end{bmatrix}
, which we can reparameterize as
\begin{bmatrix}x&y\\
xz&zy+w\end{bmatrix}
. Then we have that

\displaystyle x_{p,i}=x\quad;\quad x_{p,i+1}=y\quad;\quad x_{q,i}=xz\quad;\quad x_{q,i+1}=zy+w\;.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle dx_{p,i}=dx\quad;\quad dx_{p,i+1}=dy\quad;\quad dx_{q,i}=xdz+zdx\quad;\quad dx_{q,i+1}=ydz+zdy+dw\;.

Therefore, under these changes of variables, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{dx_{p,i}\;dx_{p,i+1}\;dx_{q,i}\;dx_{q,i+1}}{x_{p,i+1}(x_{p,i}x_{q,i+1}-x_{q,i}x_{p,i+1})x_{q,i}}=\frac{dx\;dy\;xdz\;dw}{y(xyz+xw-xyz)xz}

which simplifies to the desired result.
∎

In general, we use whichever parameterization of the two by two minors is convenient. To understand the cancelation of spurios poles, the need for a change of variables comes up in two cases. The first case involves the cancellation of the two by two minors in following three propagator configurations (see Case 3 for Theorem
4.5
):

(7)

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{P}_{1},[n])
,
(\mathcal{P}_{2},[n])
and
(\mathcal{P}_{3},[n])
be three different admissible Wilson loop diagrams that are identical except for the fact that the propagator set
\mathcal{P}_{i}
contains the pair of adjacent propagators shown in
Config i
above. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\lim_{\textrm{degree 2 factor of }R(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{i}))\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{i}))=0\;.

This proof is also given in
[
7
,
20
]
in a slightly different parametrization, and is included here for completeness.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, write
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}
with the pertinent propagators represented in the first two rows. Then the matrices
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{i})}
are identical except for the first two rows. Since the propagators are adjacent, by Lemma
A.2
we may write the first two rows as

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{1})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&a&b&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&c&d\ldots\\
1&\dots&ae&be+f&\ldots&g&h&\ldots&0&0\ldots\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{2})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}&\ldots&c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}&\ldots&0&0\ldots\\
1&\dots&0&0&\ldots&c^{\prime}e^{\prime}&d^{\prime}e^{\prime}+f^{\prime}&\ldots&g^{\prime}&h^{\prime}\ldots\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{3})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&a^{\prime\prime}&b^{\prime\prime}&\ldots&c^{\prime\prime}&d^{\prime\prime}\ldots\\
1&\dots&e^{\prime\prime}&f^{\prime\prime}&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&c^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}&d^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}+h^{\prime\prime}\ldots\end{bmatrix}\;.

We multiply the relevant rows of
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{2})}
and
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{3})}
by elements of
GL(2)
, leaving the rest of the rows unchanged. Namely, consider the products:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\frac{-e^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\frac{1}{1-e^{\prime}}\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{2})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\dots&\frac{-e^{\prime}a^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\frac{-e^{\prime}b^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\ldots&0&\frac{f^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\ldots&\frac{g^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\frac{h^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}\ldots\\
1&\ldots&a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}&\ldots&c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}&\ldots&0&0\ldots\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
\frac{-g^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\frac{1}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}\end{bmatrix}M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{3})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&e^{\prime\prime}&f^{\prime\prime}&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&c^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}&d^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}+h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}\ldots\\
1&\dots&\frac{e^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\ldots&\frac{-a^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\frac{-b^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\ldots&0&\frac{h^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}\ldots\end{bmatrix}\;.

From this, we see that, in the limit
f\rightarrow 0
and
M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{1})}
and
f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
for
\begin{bmatrix}\frac{-e^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\frac{1}{1-e^{\prime}}\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{2})}
, we have the change of variables

a=\frac{-e^{\prime}a^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}\quad;\quad b=\frac{-e^{\prime}b^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}\quad;\quad c=\frac{g^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}\quad;\quad d=\frac{d}{1-e^{\prime}}\quad;\\
\quad e=\frac{e^{\prime}-1}{e^{\prime}}\quad;\quad f=0\quad;\quad g=c^{\prime}\quad;\quad h=d^{\prime}\;.

Inverting and performing the change of variables, we see that
\lim_{f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{2}))=\lim_{f\rightarrow 0}\frac{e}{1-e}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{1}))
. A similar calculation shows that
\lim_{h^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{3}))=\lim_{f\rightarrow 0}\frac{-1}{1-e}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{1}))
. Thus, in the appropriate limit,

\displaystyle\lim_{f\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{1}))+\lim_{f^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{2}))+\lim_{h^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{3}))=0\;.\qed

\textrm{Config 4}=\hbox to96.01pt{\vbox to94.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.00545pt\lower-47.41031pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(8)

Lemma A.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{P}_{4},[n])
,
(\mathcal{P}_{5},[n])
and
(\mathcal{P}_{6},[n])
be admissible Wilson loop diagrams that are identical except for the fact that the propagator set
\mathcal{P}_{i}
contains the pair of adjacent propagators shown in
Config i
above. Let
p=(i,j)
and
q=(i,k)
with
k=j+1
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{(x_{p,i}x_{q,i+1}-x_{p,i+1},x_{q,i})\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{4}))+\lim_{x_{r,j+3}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{5}))+\lim_{x_{r,k-2}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{6}))=0\;.

Proof.

This proof follows similarly to the above. Write

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{4})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&a&b&\ldots&c&d&0&\ldots\\
1&\dots&ae&be+f&\ldots&0&g&h&\ldots\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{5})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&a^{\prime}&b^{\prime}&\ldots&c^{\prime}&d^{\prime}&0&0&\ldots\\
1&\dots&0&0&\ldots&c^{\prime}e^{\prime}&d^{\prime}e^{\prime}+f^{\prime}&g^{\prime}&h^{\prime}&\ldots\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{6})}=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&0&0&\ldots&a^{\prime\prime}&b^{\prime\prime}&c^{\prime\prime}&d^{\prime\prime}&\ldots\\
1&\dots&e^{\prime\prime}&f^{\prime\prime}&\ldots&0&0&c^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}&d^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime\prime}+h^{\prime\prime}&\ldots\end{bmatrix}\;.

We consider the change of variables defined by taking the product with

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
\frac{-e^{\prime}}{1-e^{\prime}}&\frac{1}{1-e^{\prime}}\end{bmatrix}M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{5})}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
\frac{-g^{\prime\prime}}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}&\frac{1}{1-g^{\prime\prime}}\end{bmatrix}M_{\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{P}_{6})}\;.

Then the same types of calculations as in Lemma
A.3
shows that
\lim_{a^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{6}))=\lim_{f\rightarrow 0}\frac{-1}{1-e}I(M_{\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{4})})
and
\lim_{h^{\prime}\rightarrow 0}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{5}))=\lim_{f\rightarrow 0}\frac{e}{1-e}I(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}_{4}))
, proving the result.
∎

Finally, we show that the limits defined in Lemma
A.3
and Lemma
A.4
do in fact give rise to codimension one boundaries of
\Sigma(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
. To do this, we define a more general result. In Theorem
A.8
, we show that, if
M^{\prime}
is a variable valued matrix formed by applying an invertible change of variables to a matrix
M_{\mathcal{V}}
, then sending
k
variables to
0
in
M^{\prime}
drops the dimension of the parameterized space
L(M^{\prime})
if and only if no row of
M^{\prime}
is contained in the span of any other subset of rows of
M^{\prime}
.

The result is a generalization of the equivalence of 1 and 3 in Theorem
3.5
. Condition 3 from Theorem
3.5
is analogous to the inequality from Hall’s Matching Theorem. However,
the matrices in Theorem
3.5
have the restriction that entries are either zero or totally independent. Theorem
A.8
below, relaxes this condition, allowing one to view each independent non-zero entry as a function of several variables, and setting one of these component variables to
0
, as in Lemma
A.3
and Lemma
A.4
.

We begin with a few definitions.

Definition A.5
.

For a
k\times n
variable valued matrix
M
and a set
I\subseteq[k]
, define
M_{I}
to be the matrix formed by restricting
M
to the rows
I
, and let
\mathrm{span}(M_{I})
be the subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
obtained by evaluating linear combinations of the rows
M
indexed by
I
at real parameters. Let
\mathrm{span}(M_{\emptyset})
be the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Note that these matrices are different from the variable valued matrices defined by
M_{\mathcal{V}}
above. We relax the requirement that each entry of the matrix be algebraically independent of all the others. Furthermore, while the entries of
M_{I}
are functions of independent variables, the entries themselves may be related by algebraic functions.

Definition A.6
.

Let
L(M_{I})
be the locus of the variable valued matrix
M_{I}
in
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(|I|,n)
, as given by the map in (
1
).

Example A.7
.

Let

M=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}&0&0\\
0&0&x_{1}&0\\
0&0&x_{2}&x_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

Then,
\mathrm{span}(M_{12})
consists of all points in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
whose last coordinate is zero. On the other hand,
\mathrm{span}(M_{13})
consists of points of the form
(a,b,\frac{bc}{a},c)
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
b,c\in\mathbb{R}
together with points of the form
(0,b,c,0)
. Finally,
\mathrm{span}(M_{123})
is all of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Theorem A.8
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a collection of subsets, and let
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{i,j}\}
and
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{i,j}(\mathbf{x})\}
be two sets of algebraically independent invertible variables (arising from the same indexing set
\mathcal{V}
) which are related by a change of variables. Denote by
M_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{x})
and
M_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{y})
the variable valued matrices associated to
\mathcal{V}
in the variables
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
. Let
S\subset\mathbf{x}
be a subset of the variables
\bf{x}
, and write
M^{\prime}=M_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbf{y})|_{x_{i,j}=0;x_{i,j}\in S}
. If the function
\lim_{x_{i,j}\in S\to 0}\bf{y}(\bf{x})
is invertible, denote by
d=|\mathbf{x}|-|S|
the number of variables in
\mathbf{x}
outside of the set
S
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

For all
I\subsetneq[k]
and all
j\in I^{c}
,
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{I})\subsetneq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{I\cup j})
. That is, adding a row to
M^{\prime}_{I}
always increases the size of the span.

(ii)

\mathrm{dim}(L(M^{\prime}))=d-k
.

Remark A.9
.

Similarly to how Condition 3 in Theorem
3.5
should be thought of as analogous to the inequality from Hall’s Matching Theorem, condition (i) above should be thought of as analogous to the condition from Rado’s Theorem. Rado’s Theorem is a generalization of Hall’s Theorem, which says that if
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then it is possible to select linearly independent vectors
s_{1}\in S_{1},\dots,s_{k}\in S_{k}
if and only if for all
I\subseteq[k]
,

\mathrm{dim}\left(\mathrm{span}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\right)\right)\geq|I|.

Condition (ii) from Theorem
A.8
relaxes this condition from Rado’s theorem, saying not only does any subset of vectors have the correct dimension, but adding a new vector always increases the dimension.

Proof.

We show that (ii) implies (i) via induction on
k
, the number of rows of
M_{\mathcal{V}}(\bf{x})
. When
k=1
,
L(M^{\prime})
is a parameterized subset of projective space. Since the transformation from the
\mathbf{x}
to the
\mathbf{y}
variables are invertible on
M^{\prime}=M_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbf{y})|_{x_{i,j}=0;x_{i,j}\in S}
, the matrices
M^{\prime}
and
M_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbf{x})|_{x_{i,j}=0;x_{i,j}\in S}
have the same dimension in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. One obtains the corresponding locus in projective space by scaling by a constant, so the dimensions in projective space remain the same.

For larger
k
, it is not sufficient to use the invertibility of the change of variables as an argument that the dimension does not change. While this holds on the level of matrices, it may not hold after quotienting by
GL(k)
in order to find the locus
L(M^{\prime})
.

In this proof, we consider the Grassmannian set theoretically. So
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k,\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the set of
k
-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Given a vector space
V\subsetneq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp V)
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and we write

\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k,\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp V)=\{x\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},k)|x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp V\}.

Suppose that the result holds for
k=l-1
. Let
M^{\prime}
be an
l\times n
matrix such that
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{\{1,\dots,l\}})=\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
, but that
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{I})\neq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{I\cup j})
for
j\notin I
whenever
|I|<l
. That is, condition (i) fails to hold only for the sets
|I|=l-1
.

A plane in
L(M^{\prime})
is spanned by a nonzero vector
r\in\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{l})
, plus a
l-1
plane in

L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})\cap\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp r),

i.e. an
(l-1)
-plane in
L(M^{\prime})
which is orthogonal to
r
.
Since condition (i) does not hold for
I=\{1,\dots,l-1\}
,
r\in\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
. As the map (
1
) ignores the points parameterized by
M^{\prime}_{I}
that do not have full rank, we do not need to consider the case when
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})=\mathrm{span}(r)
Observe that the set of planes in
L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
containing
r
has positive dimension.
Thus,

\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})\cap\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp r))<\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})).

Note that the subspace of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(1,\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})
obtained by evaluating
M^{\prime}
and independently taking the span of the
l
th row and of rows
1,\dots,l-1
(ignoring matrices of improper rank) is
d-k
by the inductive hypothesis. By the remarks above,
L(M^{\prime})
has strictly lower dimension than this set. So,

\dim(L(M^{\prime}))\leq d-k-1,

and thus (ii) implies (i) when
k=l
.

Next, we show that (i) implies (ii). When
k=1
,
L(M^{\prime})
is again a parameterized subset of projective space, and the argument holds similarly to the case for
k=1
in the other direction of this proof.

Suppose the result holds for
k=l-1
and let
M^{\prime}
be an
l\times n
matrix such that
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l})\neq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
. Let
d_{l}
be the number of parameters appearing in row
l
of
M^{\prime}
which do not appear in rows
1,\dots,l-1
, i.e. there are
d-d_{l}
variables in the first
l-1
rows of
M^{\prime}
. Note that
L(M^{\prime}_{l})
is
d_{l}-1
dimensional by similar arguments as the base case.

Then, for a generic point
r\in\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{l})
that comes from evaluating the last row of
M^{\prime}
, note that
r\notin\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
. So,
L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})\cap\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp r)=L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})
. In other words

\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1})\cap\mathbb{G}_{\mathbb{R}}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n}\perp r))=\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1}))=d-d_{l}-(l-1)\;,

where, by induction
\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1}))=d-d_{l}-(l-1)
. Thus,

\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l}))=(d_{l}-1)+\dim(L(M^{\prime}_{1,\dots,l-1}))=d-l.\qed

Given Theorem
A.8
, we see that the boundaries defined in Lemma
A.3
and Lemma
A.4
do in fact give rise to codimension one boundaries of
\Sigma(\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P}))
.

Corollary A.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{P},[n])
be a diagram with two propagators
p=(i,j)
and
q=(i,k)
adjacent on the edge
i
. After the reparameterization defined in Lemma
A.2
, setting
w
to zero reduces the dimension by one if and only if there is not a propagator
r=(j,k)
in
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

For a Wilson loop diagram
(\mathcal{P},[n])
, let
\bf{x}
be the parameterization that includes the variable
w
, and let
\bf{y}
be the other set of variables. Note that the Jacobian for the change of variables given in Lemma
A.2
has zero determinant when
x
is sent to
0
. When
w
is set to zero, the determinant of the Jacobian remains non-zero. Without loss of generality, assume that
w
is a variable in the row corresponding to the propagator
p
.

Further note that whether or not
r
is in
\mathcal{P}
, the diagram
(\mathcal{P}\cup r,[n])
is admissible. If
r
does not exist in
\mathcal{P}
, then adding it does not violate density. Furthermore, because
p
and
q
are adjacent, adding
r
does not violate the non-crossing condition either.

First we show that when
r\in\mathcal{P}
, when one sets
w=0
the dimension of the span of a row set does not increase with the rows included in the set. In particular, we may write
x_{q,i}=\lambda x_{p,i}
and
x_{q,i+1}=\lambda x_{p,i+1}
, with
\lambda
a real variable. By adding a scalar multiple of
M^{\prime}_{p}
to
M^{\prime}_{q}
, one gets a row that has independent variable entries exactly in the columns
V_{r}=\{j,j+1,k,k+1\}
. If
r
is in
\mathcal{P}
, the span of the rows
p
and
q
contains the span of the row
r
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{\{p,q\}})=\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{\{p,q,r\}})\;.

(9)

Thus, Theorem
A.8
implies this boundary does not have codimension one.

Next, suppose
r\not\in\mathcal{P}
. In this case, assume toward contradiction that there is a set of propagators
P\subset\mathcal{P}
such that
p\not\in P
and
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P\cup p})=\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P})
. Without loss of generality, assume that
P
one of the smallest sets of propagators such that this is true. Note that
P
cannot exist if
q\not\in P
, since all the variables in
M^{\prime}_{p}
and
M^{\prime}_{q}
are algebraically independent from the variables in
M^{\prime}_{P\setminus\{p,q\}}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, assume
q\in P
.

Since
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{p})\neq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{q})
, the equality
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P\cup p})=\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P})
implies that
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{\{p,q\}})\subseteq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P})
. But equation (
9
) means that
\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{\{p,q,r\}})\subseteq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P})
. Removing the rows
p
and
q
from both sides gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{r})\subseteq\mathrm{span}(M^{\prime}_{P\setminus q})\;.

(10)

We claim that this cannot be true. Since
q\in P
but
p
is not, we have the equality
M^{\prime}_{P\setminus q}=M_{\mathcal{V}(P\setminus q)}
because the propagator set
P\setminus q
is unaffected by the limits defining the matrix
M^{\prime}
. Note that the propagator set
(P\setminus q)\cup r
is an admissible Wilson loop diagram. By Theorem
3.5
,

\displaystyle\dim(L(\mathcal{V}((P\setminus q)\cup r)))=\dim(L(\mathcal{V}(P\setminus q)))+3,

which contradicts (
10
). Therefore, condition (i) of Theorem
A.8
must hold, and thus the limit
w\rightarrow 0
defines a codimension one subspace of
L(M_{\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{P})})
.
∎
